5	% v3 final data confirmed by Director 2026-05-18.
6	
7	\subsection{Cross-Task Validation: Event Extraction}
8	\label{sec:wikievents}
9	
10	To test generality beyond RE, we apply OntoCoDec to event extraction (EE) on WikiEvents~\cite{li2021document} (56 event types, 80 argument roles, 27 entity types, 245 role$\to$type constraints; Appendix~\ref{app:wikievents}).
11	The framework requires no modification: grammar compiles event/role vocabularies into a CFG; the semantic layer enforces role-conditioned type constraints via the same registry.
12	Violations extend to Event Vocabulary (EV), Role Vocabulary (RoV), and Argument Constraint (AC, analogous to DR).
13	
14	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
15	%  Table: WikiEvents three-model comparison (v3 final)
16	%  exp_ids: ee_{qwen,llama,mistral}_wikievents_{unconstrained,grammar_only,constrained_v3}
17	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
18	\begin{table}[t]
19	\centering
20	\small
21	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{2.8pt}
22	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
23	\begin{tabular}{@{}ll rrrr rr@{}}
24	\toprule
25	& & \multicolumn{4}{c}{\textbf{Violation Rates (\%)}}
26	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{F1}} \\
27	\cmidrule(lr){3-6} \cmidrule(l){7-8}
28	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Mode}
29	  & EV\,$\downarrow$ & RoV\,$\downarrow$ & AC & CF
30	  & Trig.\,$\uparrow$ & Ent.\,$\uparrow$ \\
31	\midrule
32	\multirow{3}{*}{Qwen3-8B}
33	  & Unconstr.\ & 24.0 & 74.9 & 0.3 & 0 & 11.8 & 0 \\
34	  & Grammar    & \textbf{0} & 76.1 & 8.2 & --- & --- & --- \\
35	  & Full S1    & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0} & 2.1 & 21.1 & 13.4 & 14.1 \\
36	\midrule
37	\multirow{3}{*}{\shortstack[l]{Llama\\3.1-8B}}
38	  & Unconstr.\ & 10.5 & 88.8 & 0.1 & 0 & 5.7 & 0 \\
39	  & Grammar    & \textbf{0} & 77.0 & 8.8 & --- & --- & --- \\
40	  & Full S1    & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0} & 3.8 & 17.2 & 7.5 & 17.6 \\
41	\midrule
42	\multirow{3}{*}{\shortstack[l]{Mistral\\7B}}
43	  & Unconstr.\ & 37.3 & 63.9 & 0 & 0 & 2.4 & 0 \\
44	  & Grammar    & \textbf{0} & 64.0 & 13.8 & --- & --- & --- \\
45	  & Full S1    & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0} & 1.3 & 14.9 & 3.9 & 11.7 \\
46	\bottomrule
47	\end{tabular}}%
48	\caption{WikiEvents EE results (246 documents, zero-shot).
49	Violation rates over parsed documents.
50	---: not applicable (grammar-only has no semantic-layer metrics).
51	F1 $\times$ 100.}
52	\label{tab:wikievents}
53	\end{table}
54	
55	\paragraph{Results.}
56	Unconstrained generation produces extreme vocabulary violations (EV+RoV exceeding 85\% combined; Table~\ref{tab:wikievents}), and all models yield entity F1\,=\,0 because the parser cannot identify well-formed mentions in off-schema output.
57	Full S1 eliminates EV and RoV across all three models ($\to$\,0\%), validating tokenizer-agnostic design across byte-level BPE (Qwen, Llama) and SentencePiece (Mistral).
58	Grammar-only ablation isolates each layer: grammar eliminates EV ($\to$\,0\%) but leaves RoV at 64--77\% because the CFG cannot constrain 80 argument roles conditioned on event context; AC rises from near-zero to 8--14\%, replicating the latent-violation surfacing pattern from DocRED (\S\ref{sec:docred-results}).
59	Full S1 then drives RoV to 0\%, confirming layer complementarity.
60	Residual AC (1.3--3.8\%) traces to confusion between semantically similar entity types (e.g., SID vs.\ SEN) where grammar-first fallback permits the incorrect type; CF rises to 15--21\%, consistent with the per-slot enforcement trade-off in RE.
61	
62	Post-hoc filtering is impractical for EE: it retains only 23/5{,}467 arguments for Qwen (99.6\% discarded), 3/5{,}053 for Llama, and 0/6{,}034 for Mistral, because unconstrained models generate generic role names rather than ontology-specific ones.
63	Unlike RE, where constraints are F1-neutral, EE shows measurable gains: entity F1 rises from 0\% to 11.7--17.6\% and trigger F1 increases by $+$1.5--1.8\,pp, because grammar enforcement produces schema-valid JSON that the parser can process.
64

1	% Section 5 — SciERC Results (Draft)
2	% Data source: Phase 2 SciERC registry experiments
3	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_grammar_only,
4	%          phase2_scierc_semantic_only, phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_scierc_full_s2
5	% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.
6	
7	\subsection{Ablation and Composability with Fine-Tuning}
8	\label{sec:scierc-results}
9	
10	Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation} presents the five-way ablation on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, 551 documents); only the active constraint layers differ across rows.
11	
12	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
13	%  Table: SciERC five-way ablation
14	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
15	\begin{table}[t]
16	\centering
17	\small
18	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}
19	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
20	\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrr rr@{}}
21	\toprule
22	& \multicolumn{5}{c}{\textbf{Violation Rates (\%)}}
23	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{F1}} \\
24	\cmidrule(lr){2-6} \cmidrule(l){7-8}
25	\textbf{Mode}
26	  & DR\,$\downarrow$ & $\Delta$DR
27	  & RV\,$\downarrow$ & ET\,$\downarrow$ & CF
28	  & Rel.\,$\uparrow$ & Ent.\,$\uparrow$ \\
29	\midrule
30	Unconstrained
31	  & 15.37 & ---
32	  & 1.39  & 0.40
33	  & 0.08
34	  & 8.23  & 31.01 \\
35	Grammar
36	  & 14.84 & $\downarrow$3\%
37	  & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0}
38	  & 0.08
39	  & 8.23  & 30.98 \\
40	Semantic
41	  & 11.65 & $\downarrow$24\%
42	  & 1.51  & 0.40
43	  & 2.71
44	  & 8.15  & 31.01 \\
45	Full (S1)
46	  & \textbf{0.93} & $\downarrow$\textbf{94\%}
47	  & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0}
48	  & 9.90
49	  & \textbf{8.36} & 30.98 \\
50	Full (S2)
51	  & 0.97  & $\downarrow$94\%
52	  & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0}
53	  & 8.03
54	  & 8.03  & \textbf{31.90} \\
55	\bottomrule
56	\end{tabular}}%
57	\caption{Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 docs, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).
58	$\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\ unconstrained.
59	Best per column in \textbf{bold} (CF excluded).
60	F1 $\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations.}
61	\label{tab:scierc-ablation}
62	\end{table}
63	
64	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
65	%  (a) Core result
66	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
67	\paragraph{Core result: DR elimination at zero F1 cost.}
68	The full two-layer constraint configuration (S1) reduces domain/range
69	violations from 15.37\% to 0.93\%, a 94\% relative reduction,
70	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093
71	while relation F1 moves from 8.23 to 8.36 ($+$0.13\,pp; bootstrap 95\% CI [$-$0.24, $+$0.58]\,pp, $p = 0.58$, 10{,}000 iterations).
72	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836
73	% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824
74	The difference is not statistically significant ($p = 0.58$), confirming
75	that constraint enforcement is F1-neutral on this dataset.
76	Post-hoc filtering achieves higher F1 in most zero-shot settings via precision gains from aggressive violation removal (SciERC: 8.63 vs.\ 8.36; DocRED: 9.61 vs.\ 8.23), but at the cost of discarding up to 48\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\ref{app:posthoc-extended}); OntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation.
77	Per-relation DR drops to $\leq$2.82\% on all types, reaching 0\% on four of seven (Appendix~\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).
78	However, constraint enforcement elevates CF from 0.08\% to 9.90\% (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
79	
80	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
81	%  (b) Grammar layer
82	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
83	\paragraph{Grammar layer: cost-free structural guarantees.}
84	Grammar-only constraints eliminate RV (1.39\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and ET (0.40\%\,$\to$\,0\%) with no F1 impact, while DR remains unchanged (15.37\%\,$\to$\,14.84\%).
85	This configuration represents the capability ceiling of grammar-based constrained decoding~\cite{geng2023gcd}, which enforces JSON structure and vocabulary membership but cannot reason about cross-field semantic constraints.
86	% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823
87	
88	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
89	%  (c) Enabling dependency
90	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
91	\paragraph{Enabling dependency.}
92	Semantic-only reduces DR by only 24\% (11.65\%) and cannot eliminate RV (1.51\%) or ET (0.40\%), confirming the \emph{enabling dependency}: without grammar-enforced vocabulary, the semantic layer encounters OOV types with no domain/range rules.
93	Adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\% reduction (11.65\%\,$\to$\,0.93\%).
94	
95	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
96	%  (d) S1 vs S2
97	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
98	\paragraph{S1 vs.\ S2.}
99	S2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\% vs.\ 9.90\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.
100	% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644
101	
102	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
103	%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning
104	%  exp_ids: lora_scierc_unconstrained, lora_scierc_full_s1
105	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
106	\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}
107	\label{sec:composability}
108	Table~\ref{tab:composability} tests composability across zero-shot vs.\ LoRA $\times$ unconstrained vs.\ S1 for Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B.
109	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_full_s1,
110	%          w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,
111	%          llama_scierc_unconstrained, llama_scierc_full_s1,
112	%          w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval
113	
114	Fine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\ref{tab:composability}).
115	Adding S1 eliminates these: Qwen DR 1.95\%\,$\to$\,0\% ($-$0.09\,pp R-F1), Llama DR 1.11\%\,$\to$\,0.09\% ($+$0.02\,pp).
116	On DocRED, the same pattern holds: Qwen DR 0.76\%\,$\to$\,0.01\% ($-$0.30\,pp), Llama DR 0.49\%\,$\to$\,0.20\% ($+$0.51\,pp).
117	% w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.01%, R-F1=40.56; w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.20%, R-F1=44.23
118	
119	Across all settings, fine-tuned + S1 yields DR\,$\leq$\,0.20\% with R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp, confirming orthogonal composability.
120	Post-fine-tuning, grammar is a no-op; the semantic layer alone reduces DR by 39--72\% (Appendix~\ref{app:ft-ablation}).
121	CF interference drops from $+$9.82\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X schema ambiguity (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-distribution}).
122	Beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates residual DR and reduces CF to $<$1\% for both Qwen and Llama (Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
123

125	\centering

Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations.}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Core result: DR elimination at zero F1 cost.}\nThe full two-layer constraint configuration (S1) reduces domain/range\nviolations from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\%, a 94\\% relative reduction,\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\nwhile relation F1 moves from 8.23 to 8.36 ($+$0.13\\,pp; bootstrap 95\\% CI [$-$0.24, $+$0.58]\\,pp, $p = 0.58$, 10{,}000 iterations).\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nThe difference is not statistically significant ($p = 0.58$), confirming\nthat constraint enforcement is F1-neutral on this dataset.\nPost-hoc filtering achieves higher F1 in most zero-shot settings via precision gains from aggressive violation removal (SciERC: 8.63 vs.\\ 8.36; DocRED: 9.61 vs.\\ 8.23), but at the cost of discarding up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}); OntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation.\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\% on all types, reaching 0\\% on four of seven (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nHowever, constraint enforcement elevates CF from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Grammar layer: cost-free structural guarantees.}\nGrammar-only constraints eliminate RV (1.39\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and ET (0.40\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) with no F1 impact, while DR remains unchanged (15.37\\%\\,$\\to$\\,14.84\\%).\nThis configuration represents the capability ceiling of grammar-based constrained decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}, which enforces JSON structure and vocabulary membership but cannot reason about cross-field semantic constraints.\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency.}\nSemantic-only reduces DR by only 24\\% (11.65\\%) and cannot eliminate RV (1.51\\%) or ET (0.40\\%), confirming the \\emph{enabling dependency}: without grammar-enforced vocabulary, the semantic layer encounters OOV types with no domain/range rules.\nAdding the grammar layer yields a further 92\\% reduction (11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2.}\nS2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\\% vs.\\ 9.90\\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: lora_scierc_unconstrained, lora_scierc_full_s1\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\nTable~\\ref{tab:composability} tests composability across zero-shot vs.\\ LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 for Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B.\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%          llama_scierc_unconstrained, llama_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval\n\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nAdding S1 eliminates these: Qwen DR 1.95\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% ($-$0.09\\,pp R-F1), Llama DR 1.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.09\\% ($+$0.02\\,pp).\nOn DocRED, the same pattern holds: Qwen DR 0.76\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.01\\% ($-$0.30\\,pp), Llama DR 0.49\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.20\\% ($+$0.51\\,pp).\n% w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.01%, R-F1=40.56; w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.20%, R-F1=44.23\n\nAcross all settings, fine-tuned + S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp, confirming orthogonal composability.\nPost-fine-tuning, grammar is a no-op; the semantic layer alone reduces DR by 39--72\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}).\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X schema ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates residual DR and reduces CF to $<$1\\% for both Qwen and Llama (Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{Zero-shot}\n    & Unconstr.\\ & 15.37 & 0.08 & 8.23  & 31.01 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.93 & 9.90 & 8.36  & 30.98 & $\\downarrow$94\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{LoRA}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{Zero-shot}\n    & Unconstr.\\ & 13.34 & 0.24 & 2.68  & 23.56 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.08 & 6.56 & 2.54  & 23.49 & $\\downarrow$99\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{LoRA}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.11 & 0    & 45.45 & 67.76 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.09 & 0.46 & 45.47 & 67.76 & $\\downarrow$92\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Composability on SciERC (551 docs): zero-shot vs.\\ LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1.\nAdding S1 to LoRA: DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.09\\%, R-F1 within $\\pm$0.1\\,pp.\nDocRED: Qwen DR $-$99\\%, Llama DR $-$59\\%.\nF1\\,$\\times$100.}\n\\label{tab:composability}\n\\end{table}\n",
      "numLines": 161,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 161
    }

59	\paragraph{Core result.}
60	Full S1 reduces DR from 18.46\% to 10.21\% ($\downarrow$45\%), eliminates ET ($\to$\,0\%), and near-eliminates RV (29.66\%\,$\to$\,0.03\%).
61	R-F1 improves from 7.74 to 8.23 ($+$0.50\,pp; $p < 0.001$), attributable to elimination of 5{,}189 OOV relation predictions.
62	CF increases modestly (0.10\%\,$\to$\,2.57\%), consistent with the CF$\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
63	
64	% exp_ids: phase2_docred_grammar_only
65	\paragraph{Grammar layer surfaces latent violations.}
66	Grammar-only constraints eliminate RV and ET but \emph{increase} DR to 26.17\% ($+$42\%): the baseline's 29.66\% OOV triples cannot be evaluated for DR; grammar enforcement assigns valid relation names whose domain/range rules the associated types frequently violate, \emph{revealing} latent incompatibilities that motivate the semantic layer.
67	
68	% exp_ids: phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1
69	\paragraph{Enabling dependency and tightness ceiling.}
70	Semantic-only reduces DR by 25\% but cannot eliminate RV (29.87\%) or prevent ET increases (2.48\%\,$\to$\,4.22\%), replicating SciERC's enabling dependency.
71	Residual DR (10.21\%) exceeds SciERC's (0.93\%) due to higher constraint tightness ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$ vs.\ 0.333), with fallback rate (10.2\%) closely tracking residual DR (Appendix~\ref{app:fallback-coverage}).
72

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Grammar-Constrained Decoding.}
5	XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\cite{willard2023outlines}, LMQL~\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql}, DOMINO~\cite{beurer-kellner2024domino}, SynCode~\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and ASAp~\cite{park2024grammar} guarantee format validity but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints such as domain/range rules.
6	
7	\paragraph{Type-Constrained Generation for IE and Code.}
8	Synchromesh~\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022}, PICARD~\cite{scholak2021picard}, and \citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} enforce type constraints in code generation; OntoCoDec migrates this to IE.
9	GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd} and GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie} constrain \emph{which tokens} appear but not \emph{which type combinations} are valid per relation.
10	DORE~\cite{vukovic2024dore} uses generate-then-select with confidence thresholds; GenIE~\cite{josifoski2022genie} constrains entities via a trie without relation-specific type enforcement; EnriCo~\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} applies ASP constraints with task-specific manually authored rules.
11	Training-based methods~\cite{paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,lu2022uie,knowcoder_acl2024,li2023codeie} provide only soft guidance; post-hoc approaches~\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} validate after generation rather than preventing violations.
12	
13	\paragraph{Quality Impact.}
14	CRANE~\cite{banerjee2025crane} and \citet{schall2025hiddencost} show grammar restrictions can degrade reasoning, but \citet{tam2024letmespeak} find extraction tasks robust to constraints.
15	OntoCoDec confirms this: DR decreases 42--99\% with zero F1 degradation across three model families (extended survey in Appendix~\ref{app:related_work}).
16	
```

      "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Grammar-Constrained Decoding.}\nXGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\\cite{willard2023outlines}, LMQL~\\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql}, DOMINO~\\cite{beurer-kellner2024domino}, SynCode~\\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and ASAp~\\cite{park2024grammar} guarantee format validity but cannot enforce cross-field semantic constraints such as domain/range rules.\n\n\\paragraph{Type-Constrained Generation for IE and Code.}\nSynchromesh~\\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022}, PICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard}, and \\citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} enforce type constraints in code generation; OntoCoDec migrates this to IE.\nGCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd} and GoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie} constrain \\emph{which tokens} appear but not \\emph{which type combinations} are valid per relation.\nDORE~\\cite{vukovic2024dore} uses generate-then-select with confidence thresholds; GenIE~\\cite{josifoski2022genie} constrains entities via a trie without relation-specific type enforcement; EnriCo~\\cite{zaratiana2024enrico} applies ASP constraints with task-specific manually authored rules.\nTraining-based methods~\\cite{paolini2021tanl,cabot2021rebel,lu2022uie,knowcoder_acl2024,li2023codeie} provide only soft guidance; post-hoc approaches~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} validate after generation rather than preventing violations.\n\n\\paragraph{Quality Impact.}\nCRANE~\\cite{banerjee2025crane} and \\citet{schall2025hiddencost} show grammar restrictions can degrade reasoning, but \\citet{tam2024letmespeak} find extraction tasks robust to constraints.\nOntoCoDec confirms this: DR decreases 42--99\\% with zero F1 degradation across three model families (extended survey in Appendix~\\ref{app:related_work}).\n",
      "numLines": 16,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 16
    }
  },

1	% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion (maximally compressed)
2	% Full discussion in Appendix; Mistral boundary condition in app:dual-factor.
3	
4	OntoCoDec compiles IE ontology schemas into composable token-level decoding constraints, achieving 42--99\% DR reduction across three models and three datasets (2 RE + 1 EE) with no significant RE F1 cost; in EE, format compliance additionally enables entity extraction (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}).
5	Composed with LoRA fine-tuning, DR reaches $\leq$\,0.20\% at $<$0.6\,pp F1 cost; residual DR follows a dual-factor pattern ($\bar{\kappa} \times \text{RV}_{\text{baseline}}$; Appendix~\ref{app:dual-factor}).
6	CF impact is model-dependent (up to 12\% for calibrated models, reduced for poorly-calibrated ones); fine-tuning resolves this tension (CF\,$\leq$\,1.2\%; \S\ref{sec:composability}), and beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates both residual DR and CF for calibrated models (Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
7	Cross-field consistency remains an open challenge for per-slot enforcement; global entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF without trading it against DR.
8	
```

```text
1	\section{Reproducibility Details}
2	\label{app:reproducibility}
3	
4	\paragraph{Fine-tuning configuration.}
5	All four fine-tuned models (Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on SciERC and DocRED) use identical LoRA hyperparameters: rank $r{=}16$, $\alpha{=}32$, dropout $0.05$, no bias, targeting all attention and MLP projection layers (\texttt{q\_proj}, \texttt{k\_proj}, \texttt{v\_proj}, \texttt{o\_proj}, \texttt{gate\_proj}, \texttt{up\_proj}, \texttt{down\_proj}).
6	Training uses AdamW with learning rate $2{\times}10^{-4}$, cosine schedule, warmup ratio $0.1$, effective batch size $16$, bf16 precision, gradient checkpointing, and completion-only loss for 3 epochs.
7	SciERC trains on 1,762 examples (260 dev) for 333 steps; DocRED trains on 3,053 examples (998 dev) for 573 steps.
8	Maximum sequence length is 2,048 (SciERC) and 4,096 (DocRED).
9	All runs use seed 42.
10	
11	\paragraph{Hardware and software.}
12	Training runs on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (98\,GB VRAM).
13	SciERC fine-tuning takes approximately 11 minutes per model; DocRED takes approximately 1.5 hours.
14	Software versions: PyTorch 2.10, Transformers 4.57, PEFT 0.19, TRL 1.1, Accelerate 1.13.
15	
16	\paragraph{Inference time.}
17	Table~\ref{tab:inference-time} reports per-document inference times for Qwen3-8B under zero-shot greedy decoding across constraint configurations.
18	
19	\begin{table}[h]
20	\centering
21	\small
22	\caption{Inference time (seconds/document, mean $\pm$ std) for Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, greedy decoding.}
23	\label{tab:inference-time}
24	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
25	\toprule
26	\textbf{Configuration} & \textbf{SciERC} & \textbf{DocRED} \\
27	\midrule
28	Unconstrained       & $8.69 \pm 3.89$  & $19.75 \pm 8.99$  \\
29	Grammar-only        & $8.81 \pm 4.51$  & $20.58 \pm 10.75$ \\
30	Semantic-only       & $7.23 \pm 3.91$  & $20.53 \pm 9.37$  \\
31	Full S1             & $6.76 \pm 3.67$  & $20.39 \pm 9.43$  \\
32	Post-hoc            & $8.69 \pm 3.89$  & $19.78 \pm 8.99$  \\
33	\bottomrule
34	\end{tabular}
35	\end{table}
36	
37	On SciERC, full S1 constrained decoding is 22\% \emph{faster} than unconstrained generation because suppressing invalid tokens reduces output length.
38	On DocRED (longer documents), the masking computation adds a small constant overhead (+3.2\%) that slightly exceeds the token-savings effect.
39	Grammar-only and post-hoc filtering add negligible overhead ($<$4.2\% and $<$0.2\%, respectively).
40	
```

</details>

```text
Perfect! Here's a concise summary of the official ACL ARR review form criteria and scoring dimensions for 2025-2026:

## Official ACL ARR Review Form Dimensions

### Core Scoring Dimensions (1-5 scales):

1. **Soundness** (1-5)
   - 1 = Major Issues
   - 5 = Excellent
   - Must be justified by review text; scores must reflect technical rigor and claim support

2. **Excitement** (1-5)
   - 1 = Not Exciting
   - 5 = Highly Exciting
   - Subjective assessment; orthogonal to soundness
   - Includes innovation, transformational potential, surprising results, conceptual breakthroughs

3. **Overall Recommendation** (1-5)
   - 1 = Do not resubmit
   - 2 = Resubmit next cycle
   - 1.5 = Resubmit after next cycle
   - 4 = Conference
   - 5 = Consider for Award
   - Composite reflecting soundness, excitement, novelty, and impact

4. **Reviewer Confidence** (1-5)
   - 1 = Not my area
   - 5 = Positive that my evaluation is correct

### Additional Evaluation Criteria:

- **Reproducibility** (1-5 scale)
- **Dataset Value** (1-5 scale)
- **Software Utility** (1-5 scale)
- **Author Anonymity Knowledge** (1-5 scale)
- Limitations and societal impact discussion
- Ethical concerns alignment with ACL Code of Ethics

### Key Principles:
- Soundness and excitement are **orthogonal** dimensions
- All papers must meet soundness and reproducibility standards for Findings/conference acceptance
- Excitement is subjective and can include transformation, novelty, and impact considerations
- Reviews should provide specific, justified feedback rather than vague criticisms

Sources:
- [ACL Rolling Review Form](http://aclrollingreview.org/reviewform)
- [ARR Reviewer Guidelines](http://aclrollingreview.org/reviewerguidelines)
- [CALL FOR PAPERS – ACL Rolling Review](http://aclrollingreview.org/cfp)
```

Looking through the abstract line by line, I'm tallying up the word counts. Line 2 introduces OntoCoDec and describes the two composable token-level decoding constraints at around 24 words. Line 5 explains how the layers compose together at 18 words. Line 7 presents the problem statement about LLM-based IE violations with specific percentages across datasets at 35 words. Line 8 begins discussing how OntoCoDec reduces these violations.

The next section shows that OntoCoDec achieves 42-99% reduction in domain/range violations across three different model families without meaningful F1 degradation. For the primary model tested, the improvements are modest but statistically significant on DocRED while remaining non-significant on SciERC. The analysis reveals that residual violations in calibrated models follow a predictable pattern based on schema characteristics and baseline violation rates. When combined with LoRA fine-tuning, the approach pushes violation rates down to nearly negligible levels on both datasets.

Integrating beam search into the full pipeline eliminates constraint violations entirely while maintaining extraction quality. The framework also transfers effectively to event extraction tasks, completely removing vocabulary violations and even recovering entity extraction performance in cases where unconstrained models fail. There's an interesting trade-off emerging between cross-field consistency and domain/range violations when enforcing constraints per-relation independently. I'm verifying that all abbreviations in the abstract are properly expanded on first use. Looking at the introduction, the distinction between IE and type-constrained code generation is clearly articulated—IE requires dynamic entity registries and stateful cross-field constraints that depend on co-occurring relations, unlike static type declarations in traditional code generation. 6. WikiEvents entity F1 improvements check out: the range of 11.7-17.6% across models matches the stated 12-18%. ✅
7. Now verifying the zero-shot composability fraction claims in Table 3.
8. For fine-tuning, the Qwen SciERC LoRA composability fraction of 1.18% aligns with the abstract's ≤1.2% threshold, though I need to confirm the DocRED fine-tuned values aren't shown in the composability table itself.
9. The DR reduction range of 42-99% is confirmed: SciERC achieves 94-99% while DocRED ranges from 42-76% depending on the model.

Checking fine-tuning composability fractions against the abstract claim of ≤1.2%—Qwen SciERC LoRA shows 1.18%, which fits, though DocRED fine-tuned values aren't visible in the composability table. The DR reduction range of 42-99% holds up: SciERC hits 94-99% while DocRED varies from 42-76% across models.

Now I'm looking at the section structure for composability results—SciERC has its ablation and composability subsection, and I need to check what DocRED's section is titled. The DocRED composability results are embedded in the caption itself rather than displayed in the table, showing percentage changes for both models. The section title has been corrected, but the data presentation is still incomplete—it needs to be moved into an actual table format rather than staying as inline text in the caption.

Now checking the reproducibility details in the appendix: the LoRA configuration is fully specified with rank, alpha, dropout, and target layers, and the training setup includes all necessary parameters like learning rate, schedule, batch size, and precision. The GPU specs and seed information are documented, though the seeds section appears to be cut off. I'm checking the DocRED results where grammar mode achieves 0.03% residual violation rate, but I don't see any explanation in the draft for why it's not zero—this gap needs to be addressed in the paper.

Now I'm turning to the core novelty assessment. The paper claims to be the first to compile IE ontology schemas into decoding-time semantic constraints through dynamic logit masking that enforces domain and range types. Looking at related work, grammar-constrained decoding systems like XGrammar and Outlines handle structural constraints only, while DORE uses post-hoc filtering rather than during-decoding enforcement, GenIE applies trie constraints for entities without relation-specific type checking, and EnriCo...

Actually, let me reconsider the full landscape here. There's work on constrained decoding across multiple domains—ASP constraints for joint extraction, incremental parsing for SQL with fixed schemas, semantic decoding for code generation, and even full type systems compiled to prefix automata. The paper's actual contribution is adapting type-constrained decoding from code generation to IE, which introduces three distinct technical challenges: building a dynamic entity registry during generation rather than relying on static declarations, inferring types from context, and handling constraints that shift based on what's been generated so far. This addresses the earlier concern about whether it's just a straightforward migration by showing the specific technical innovations needed.

The core technical contributions are solid: dynamically constructing the entity registry as generation proceeds, using pair-based sequential masking that accounts for non-rectangular constraints, composing grammar and semantic information with dependency tracking, and applying continuation masking to enforce types regardless of tokenizer. It's a thoughtful engineering effort with solid empirical results, though the novelty is incremental rather than a fundamental algorithmic advance.

On the writing side, the em-dashes are gone and the prose is cleaner overall. The paragraph-heavy structure is typical for compressed conference papers. The abstract runs long though—closer to 280-320 words when you account for the LaTeX, well over the typical 150-250 word target.

Now I'm noticing several other potential issues: the composability results are only shown in a table for SciERC while DocRED results are scattered in the text, the Related Work section is quite compressed at just 15 lines, and the Limitations section is unusually detailed with 13 separate points. There's also no fine-tuning results on WikiEvents—only zero-shot evaluation, which weakens the generalization claims. Estimating page count from the structure suggests the paper is running long across most sections. I'm mapping out the actual paper structure now—Constraint Composition is its own section, and Experiments has five subsections covering different datasets and analyses. With Introduction, Background, Method, Related Work, Discussion, plus Limitations, Ethics, References, and a nine-section appendix, this is definitely pushing past the eight-page limit.

Let me verify the abstract word count by carefully reading through the rendered text to see how much space it's actually taking up.

Now I'm looking at the empirical results—the baseline violation rates are around 15-18% across the two datasets, and OntoCoDec achieves substantial reductions of 42-99% depending on the model. The F1 scores show minimal degradation, with gains on DocRED and negligible changes on SciERC. There's an interesting pattern emerging where the residual violations correlate with schema tightness and baseline rates, suggesting the constraint effectiveness is somewhat predictable from the model and schema characteristics.

When combined with LoRA fine-tuning, the violation rates drop dramatically to under 0.2%, and with beam search added on top, the system achieves zero violations across all categories while maintaining extraction quality. The approach also transfers cleanly to event extraction tasks without any architectural changes, completely eliminating vocabulary violations and even enabling entity extraction in cases where unconstrained models fail entirely. However, there's a trade-off emerging between per-relation enforcement and cross-field consistency—enforcing constraints independently can introduce new consistency violations in some cases.

Now I'm reviewing the abstract length and checking for stylistic issues. The current version is running a bit long at around 290-310 words, which exceeds the typical 150-250 word guideline. I'm also noticing some patterns in the phrasing that could be tightened up, particularly around the use of trailing -ing constructions. Let me identify the specific instances and plan revisions accordingly.

I've confirmed the paper has the review package enabled, proper anonymization, and line numbers are active. Rather than continue searching for every -ing phrase variation, I should move forward with drafting the review based on what I've already identified.

Looking at the overall contribution, this is solid empirical work that tackles a genuine problem in LLM-based information extraction through a well-engineered solution with comprehensive evaluation across multiple models and datasets. The core limitation is novelty—adapting type-constrained decoding from code generation to IE is a reasonable engineering contribution, but it's unclear whether that's sufficiently novel for a top venue. The paper also has some presentation issues like an oversized abstract and a compressed related work section, plus the zero-shot baseline numbers are concerningly low, which raises questions about the problem formulation itself. I'm leaning toward a borderline accept around 3.5, though the novelty question is really the deciding factor. The dual-factor pattern is an interesting finding, and the non-rectangularity analysis adds a nice technical dimension. The zero-shot results are quite weak (2-8% R-F1), which makes me question how practically relevant that setting is, though the fine-tuned numbers (43-45% R-F1) are more reasonable and still benefit from the constraints. Before finalizing the review, I'm checking whether each contribution listed in the introduction maps to corresponding sections in the paper—the systematic violation study, the OntoCoDec framework with composition analysis, and the empirical validation all appear to be properly covered.

I'm also verifying that all figures and tables are referenced in the text and that the Limitations section appears in the correct location before the bibliography, which it does. There's a WikiEvents appendix referenced as well, so everything seems properly structured and ready for the final review.

alone cannot eliminate, specifically on tight-constraint relations where the valid type-pair space is small.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_discussion.tex:298:The implication is twofold: (i)~the irreducible floor from schema gaps is small ($\\sim$3 violations across 4 settings), confirming that the constraint system operates near its theoretical optimum; (ii)~residual violations from entity typing errors are addressable through stronger base models or improved entity typing modules, without modifying the constraint architecture.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_relwork_additions.tex:16:In the text-to-SQL domain, PICARD~\\cite{scholak2021picard} maintains an incremental SQL parsing state that rejects tokens violating syntax or referencing non-existent database columns at each decoding step, achieving state-of-the-art results on Spider and CoSQL without modifying model weights.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_relwork_additions.tex:42:RELATE~\\cite{olasunkanmi2025relate} addresses the alignment gap between LLM-extracted biomedical relations and standardized ontology predicates through a three-stage pipeline: ontology preprocessing with predicate embeddings, similarity-based retrieval via SapBERT, and LLM-based reranking with negation handling, achieving 94\\% accuracy@10 on ChemProt.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_relwork_outline.tex:10:XGrammar~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar} partitions the vocabulary into context-independent and context-dependent tokens, achieving 100$\\times$ speedup over prior CFG-constrained decoding via persistent pushdown automata.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_relwork_outline.tex:28:Synchromesh~\\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022} pioneers Constrained Semantic Decoding (CSD), which enforces syntax, type, and scope constraints simultaneously during code generation from pre-trained LMs, establishing that semantic constraints can be compiled into token-level filtering without fine-tuning.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_relwork_outline.tex:30:MGD~\\cite{mgd_neurips2023} uses static-analysis monitors to perform type checking at decode time, enabling SantaCoder-1.1B to outperform text-davinci-003 on repository-level code completion by enforcing type-correct identifier usage.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_relwork_outline.tex:69:GoLLIE~\\cite{sainz2024gollie} expresses annotation guidelines as Python dataclass definitions, fine-tuning Code-LLaMA to generate typed code, achieving strong zero-shot transfer to unseen IE schemas.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_relwork_outline.tex:75:ADELIE~\\cite{qi2024adelie} uses SFT and DPO to align LLMs on IE tasks, achieving SOTA among open-source models while preserving general capabilities.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_relwork_outline.tex:90:ODKE+~\\cite{khorshidi2025odke} dynamically generates ontology snippets containing domain/range constraints as prompt context, achieving 98.8\\% precision across 195 predicates at web scale---but constraint adherence relies on prompt-level guidance, not enforcement.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section6_relwork_outline.tex:92:SPIRES/OntoGPT~\\cite{spires_2024} 1	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section5_docred_draft_9b84f1.tex:179:for the model to redirect probability mass toward, enabling
2	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section1_introduction_draft.tex:22:The layers compose via bitwise AND with an \emph{enabling dependency}: the grammar layer guarantees vocabulary membership, enabling the semantic layer to look up domain/range rules.
3	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section1_introduction_draft.tex:28:    \item The first systematic study of ontological constraint violations in LLM-based IE (spanning per-relation tightness analysis, dual-factor characterization, and post-hoc baseline comparison), showing that violation rates are driven by constraint tightness rather than ontology size, and that prompt-level intervention is per-relation unreliable.
4	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_doc_length.tex:67:Longer documents produce more violations in the unconstrained setting (e.g., DocRED long-bin: 3,550 vs.\ short-bin: 2,445), yet OntoCoDec achieves proportionally similar reduction, confirming that the constraint framework scales reliably with document complexity.
5	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:9:XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar} partitions the vocabulary into context-independent and context-dependent tokens and maintains persistent pushdown automata across decoding steps, achieving up to 100$\times$ speedup over prior CFG-constrained decoding methods.
6	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:10:Outlines~\cite{willard2023outlines} reformulates constrained generation as finite-state machine transitions with offline vocabulary indexing, enabling efficient enforcement of regular-expression and JSON-schema constraints without runtime overhead per token.
7	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:11:LMQL~\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql} introduces a declarative query language that compiles type, length, and regex constraints into token-level masks, providing a programmable interface for specifying multi-variable output structures.
8	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:14:Together, these advances have made grammar-constrained decoding both fast and statistically principled, establishing a robust foundation for structured generation.
9	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:22:In programming-language generation, constrained decoding has been extended beyond syntax to enforce semantic properties such as type correctness, demonstrating that richer-than-CFG constraints can be compiled into token-level filtering.
10	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:23:In the text-to-SQL domain, PICARD~\cite{scholak2021picard} maintains an incremental SQL parsing state that rejects tokens violating syntax or referencing non-existent database columns at each decoding step, achieving state-of-the-art results on Spider and CoSQL without modifying model weights.
11	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:26:Synchromesh~\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022} pioneers Constrained Semantic Decoding (CSD), which enforces syntax, type, and scope constraints simultaneously during code generation from pre-trained LMs without fine-tuning, establishing that semantic constraints can be expressed as dynamic token masks conditioned on the generation prefix.
12	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:27:MGD~\cite{mgd_neurips2023} uses static-analysis monitors to perform incremental type checking at decode time, enabling SantaCoder-1.1B to outperform text-davinci-003 on repository-level code completion by ensuring that every generated identifier is type-correct with respect to the surrounding code context.
13	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:40:\citet{roth_yih_2004} formulate joint entity and relation identification as an integer linear program (ILP) that enforces type constraints (e.g., \textsc{Emp-Org} only between \textsc{Per} and \textsc{Org} entities), establishing the foundational principle that relational type compatibility should be a hard constraint, not a soft preference learned from data.
14	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:42:GenIE~\cite{josifoski2022genie} extends this approach to end-to-end information extraction with state-based constraints that enforce valid triple structures, achieving strong performance on closed-world knowledge base population, though it requires task-specific fine-tuning on the target schema.
15	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:56:GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie} expresses annotation guidelines as Python dataclass definitions and fine-tunes Code-LLaMA to generate typed code, achieving strong zero-shot transfer to unseen IE schemas by leveraging the implicit type structure of programming languages.
16	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:59:ADELIE~\cite{qi2024adelie} uses SFT and DPO to align LLMs on IE tasks, achieving state-of-the-art results among open-source models while preserving general capabilities, though compliance with ontological constraints remains a learned soft preference rather than a guarantee.
17	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:72:ODKE+~\cite{khorshidi2025odke} dynamically generates ontology snippets containing domain/range constraints as prompt context, achieving 98.8\% precision across 195 predicates at web scale, but constraint adherence relies on prompt-level guidance, not enforcement, leaving residual violations unaddressed.
18	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:73:SPIRES/OntoGPT~\cite{spires_2024} provides an ontology-based extraction framework that grounds LLM outputs against OBO ontologies post-generation, validating extracted entities and relations through ontology lookup and correcting mismatches in a second pass, representing the prompt-then-validate paradigm in the biomedical domain.
19	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:77:RELATE~\cite{olasunkanmi2025relate} addresses the alignment gap between LLM-extracted biomedical relations and standardized ontology predicates through a three-stage pipeline: ontology preprocessing with predicate embeddings, similarity-based retrieval via SapBERT, and LLM-based reranking with negation handling, achieving 94\% accuracy@10 on ChemProt.
20	/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:83:Post-hoc validation detects violations after generation, requiring rejection or correction cycles that waste compute. In our setting, 15.37\% of generated triples contain domain/range violations under unconstrained decoding, representing substantial wasted generation effort.
